## Page 1


STATIONARY VLASOV-POISSON-BOLTZMANN SYSTEM IN A CONVEX DOMAIN
HONGXU CHEN AND JIAXIN JIN
Abstract. We study the stationary and dynamical Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system in a bounded, convex
domain subject to a confining external potential field. For the stationary problem, we construct a unique
stationary solution with an inflow boundary condition. A key difficulty is to obtain pointwise regularity for
stationary solutions due to the intricate coupling between the self-consistent electric field and the Boltzmann
collision operator. To overcome this issue, we establish a W 1,p
x,v –αC1
x,v bootstrap framework and derive an
unweighted C1
v estimate by exploiting the structure of the external potential field. We then investigate the
dynamical Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system near the stationary solution. We prove the global existence and
uniqueness of solutions for small perturbations and establish exponential convergence toward the stationary
state in weighted L∞norms. Our results reveal the stabilizing effect of the external potential field and
provide a framework for the stationary and dynamical theories of the Vlasov-Poisson-Boltzmann system in
bounded domains.

1. Introduction
The Vlasov–Poisson–Boltzmann (VPB) system is a fundamental kinetic model describing the evolution of
dilute charged particles under self-consistent electrostatic interactions and binary collisions [3,11,18,19,24,34].
1
arXiv:2606.04960v1  [math.AP]  3 Jun 2026


## Page 2


2
H. CHEN AND J. JIN
It plays a central role in plasma physics and kinetic theory, combining two main mechanisms: nonlinear
Boltzmann collisions and self-consistent electrostatic forces governed by the Poisson equation.
In this paper, we investigate the stationary VPB system in a three-dimensional convex and bounded
domain Ω⊂R3. The stationary Vlasov-Boltzmann equation is formulated as
v · ∇xFs −E · ∇vFs = Q(Fs, Fs),
(x, v) ∈Ω× R3.
(1.1)
Here, Fs = Fs(x, v) ≥0 stands for the stationary velocity distribution function of the charged particles with
velocity v = (v1, v2, v3) ∈R3 at position x ∈Ω, the boundary conditions are to be specified later. E stands
for the field. The Boltzmann collision term Q is a bilinear integral operator acting only on the velocity
variable, and in this manuscript, we only consider the hard sphere model, reading as
Q(F, G) :=
ˆ
R3
ˆ
S2 |(v −u) · ω|[F(u′)G(v′) −F(u)G(v)] dωdu,
where the velocity pairs (v, u) and (v′, u′) are given in the ω-representation by
v′ = v + [(u −v) · ω]ω,
u′ = u −[(u −v) · ω]ω,
ω ∈S2,
which corresponds to conservation of momentum and energy for elastic collisions between molecules:
v + u = v′ + u′,
|v|2 + |u|2 = |v′|2 + |u′|2.
It is well known that the equilibrium state of the Boltzmann equation is given by Maxwellians, which we
denote by
µ(v) :=
1
(2π)3/2 e−|v|2
2 .
The mathematical theory of kinetic equations of Boltzmann and Vlasov type has been extensively de-
veloped.
In the whole space or torus setting, global perturbative theories near Maxwellians have been
established through high-order energy methods, velocity weights, and hypocoercivity structures, including
the Boltzmann equation, VPB system, and Vlasov-Maxwell-Boltzmann system [13, 20–22, 35]. In bounded
domains, however, the presence of boundaries introduces additional difficulties. The transport dynamics
interact with boundary conditions (inflow, specular reflection, or diffuse reflection), leading to possible loss
of regularity near grazing trajectories. To resolve this issue, a low-regularity space has to be considered
for well-posedness. In [23], Guo proposed the L2 −L∞framework to construct a unique solution to the
Boltzmann equation that converges to the equilibrium exponentially fast, with several boundary conditions.
Later, Esposito-Guo-Kim-Marra applied the L2 −L∞argument and constructed a unique stationary Boltz-
mann solution with dynamical stability under non-isothermal boundary in [15,16]. We refer to [4,12,31] for
further developments under different boundary conditions.
In the bounded domain, the regularity issue remains unknown in the L2 −L∞framework.
In fact,
the geometry of the domain plays a crucial role, for instance, in the non-convex domain, singularity may
propagates into the interior domain through characteristics, leading to discontinuity, thus BV regularity
becomes the optimal regularity as demonstrated in [25, 30]. Even in a convex domain, the regularity issue
is challenging due to the mixing of the singularity along the characteristic and the velocities interchange
induced by the nonlocal Boltzmann operator. A major breakthrough was achieved by Guo-Kim-Tonon-
Trescases in [26], where a kinetic weight was proposed to compensate the singularity, and the weight C1
estimate was achieved under several boundary condition in a strictly convex domain. This work inspired
further developments in the regularity theory for stationary problems, including the linear problem studied
in [8,9], and the nonlinear problem with higher C1,β regularity and Sobolev regularity in [5,6]. The stationary
regularity estimate provides important methodology for the investigation of the stationary VPB system in the
current manuscript. We also mentioned recent progress on regularity in non-convex domains with specular
boundary conditions in [1,32].
For the dynamical VPB system in bounded domains, a recent advancement was achieved by Cao-Kim-
Lee in [3], who constructed a unique global-in-time solution near equilibrium in convex domains with diffuse
boundary conditions. In particular, they derived weighted W 1,p regularity for p < 6 to establish the existence
and uniqueness. Additional studies on the VPB system under different boundary conditions and potential
settings can be found in [7,33]. To refine the weighted W 1,p regularity, Cao imposed an external potential
field with a favorable sign condition on the boundary in [2], and constructed a local solution with the weighted
C1 regularity. This external potential field can enhance the regularity by preventing characteristics from


## Page 3


STATIONARY VLASOV-POISSON-BOLTZMANN SYSTEM IN A CONVEX DOMAIN
3
becoming tangential to the boundary. A similar type of external field was adopted for the Vlasov–Poisson
system in the earlier work [27]. We further note the dominant gravity field considered in [28,29] also exhibits
a similar regularity-enhancing effect.
Despite these developments, the well-posedness of the stationary VPB system remains an open problem.
The stationary problem is more challenging than the time-dependent one. In time-dependent settings, time
decay provides a natural mechanism to control nonlinear growth, while the Gronwall-type argument can
be applied to study the regularity estimate and the stability estimate, such as in [3]. Moreover, the global
well-posedness can be justified through a combination of global a priori estimate and local well-posedness
argument. In contrast, stationary systems lack time dissipation and Gronwall’s inequality, and one must rely
on structural coercivity, elliptic coupling through the Poisson equation, and precise control of the Vlasov
operator to construct the regularity and well-posedness. These difficulties are particularly pronounced in
three-dimensional domains, which are also the most relevant setting for physical applications.
In this manuscript, we consider the stationary Vlasov–Poisson–Boltzmann system in a three-dimensional,
strictly convex, bounded domain with an external potential field. As a first attempt at the stationary VPB
problem, we focus on inflow boundary conditions in this work, while leaving the diffuse reflection boundary
condition for future work. The main results of this paper are as follows:
(1) Construction of a unique stationary solution near the Maxwellian equilibrium.
(2) Dynamical stability of the stationary solution.
In the remainder of this introduction, we present the basic setting of the problem, state the main results
for both the stationary and dynamical systems, and discuss the main difficulties and proof strategy. Finally,
we provide an outline of the paper and introduce the notation used throughout the paper.

system in the phase space (x, v) ∈Ω× R3, where Ω⊂R3 is the strictly convex and bounded domain
introduced in Section 1.1, in the presence of an external potential field ϕE. By substituting E in (1.1) with
the self-consistent electric field and the external potential field, the problem is formulated as

Here, ∇xϕFs represents the self-consistent electric field generated from the stationary charged particles. It
can be determined by solving the Poisson equation with the zero Dirichlet boundary condition.
In the
Poisson equation, ρ0 represents the background density, and we assume the doping profile is given by ρ0e−ϕE
under the influence of the external potential ϕE. Without loss of generality, we normalize ρ0 ≡1. Moreover,
for the external potential ϕE, we impose the favorable sign condition −∂nϕE > CE > 0 on the boundary.

Fs = e−ϕEµ when Fb = e−ϕEµ.

Fb = e−ϕEµ + e−ϕE/2√µfb.
Accordingly, we seek a stationary solution of the form

Remark 3. Without the external field, the weighted C1 regularity in (1.15) is not expected in general, as
discussed in [3]. Indeed, the variance of the kinetic weight
α2
h ∼|n(x) · v|2 + dist(x, ∂Ω)|v|2
along the characteristic becomes singular in the small-velocity regime |v| ≪1 due to the electric field. In the
presence of an external potential field, [2] incorporated the distance information and introduced the weight
(1.12), thereby establishing local well-posedness with weighted C1 regularity.
In this paper, we adapt this weight and further construct a unique stationary solution with weighted C1
regularity. This regularity is particularly delicate in the stationary setting, and W 1,p estimates are required
to implement the bootstrap argument; see the detailed discussion in Section 1.4. In addition, we establish C1
v
regularity without kinetic weight, which plays an essential role in proving dynamical stability and deriving
uniform-in-time estimates; see also Remark 5 for further discussion.

Remark 5. For the pure Boltzmann equation, the regularity is shown to decay exponentially fast in [6].
However, in the presence of the additional Vlasov–Poisson coupling, the term ∇vh remains on the right-
hand side of the perturbation equation (1.17). Due to the lack of control of second-order velocity derivatives
of h, it is necessary to adopt an alternative formulation. In particular, we rewrite the solution F(t, x, v) as
F(t, x, v) = e−ϕEµ + e−ϕE/2√µf.
This yields a reformulated system (8.17) in which the term involving ∇vh is replaced by derivatives of the
Maxwellian ∇v√µ. On the other hand, this formulation leads to a non-homogeneous boundary condition
f(t, x, v)|γ−= fb,
and therefore decay in time for ∂x,vf cannot be expected. Consequently, the dynamical regularity result in
(1.21) is only uniformly bounded in time, rather than decaying exponentially.
In addition, due to the presence of the term ∇vh on the right-hand side, a C1
v estimate ∥∇vh∥L∞
x,v is
required when applying the L2–L∞framework to prove dynamical stability. For the pure Boltzmann equation,
the velocity derivative typically exhibits a singularity of order 1/|v|2, as shown in [5]. Such a C1
v estimate
can only be recovered in the presence of the external potential field ∇xϕE. A similar type of C1
v estimate was
also used in [29], where a dominant gravitational field is considered for the Vlasov–Poisson system. For a
different type of stabilizing effect via an external electric field in the Vlasov–Poisson system, see [14].
1.4. Main difficulties and proof strategy. Due to the presence of the self-consistent electric field, the
main difficulty in constructing a unique stationary solution to the VPB system lies in obtaining suitable
stationary regularity estimates. The existence and uniqueness arguments rely on these estimates together
with a sequential construction scheme. The dynamical problem is closely related and follows similar ideas; for
this reason, we mainly focus on the stationary case in the discussion of the key difficulties. The corresponding
differences and additional ideas in the dynamical setting are described in Remark 5
• A priori W 1,p estimate
In the stationary problem, a major difficulty in deriving stationary regularity estimates lies in controlling
the integral operator K and its associated kernel k (see Section 2.2). For the stationary Boltzmann equation,
the key idea in [5] is to exploit the mixing effect along characteristics to gain regularity. Specifically, a double
Duhamel formula is employed, leading to the expression

∇xh(x −(t −s)v −(s −s′)u, u′) = −(s −s′)−1∇u[h(x −(t −s)v −(s −s′)u, u′)],
the derivative can be removed through integration by parts in du when s −s′ is away from 0.
In our stationary VPB problem, the characteristic is determined by the potential field, and the Duhamel

We note that the W 1,p estimate in [6] is derived from a weighted point-wise C1 estimate. In our setting,
the W 1,p regularity must be constructed independently. Due to the absence of a Gronwall inequality in the
stationary problem, the W 1,p estimate is constructed directly through the method of characteristics. In this
framework, the problematic term (1.22) can be controlled under the Lp norm after integration by parts. The
integrability in v within Lp
v arises from carefully applying the H¨older inequality to extract ⟨v⟩−1 from the
damping factor ν(v) in time integration and the integration against k(v, u)du. This imposes the condition
p > 2, which holds in convex domains. Another difficulty in the W 1,p estimate arises from the singularity of
the derivative of the backward exit time tb (see (2.2)), given by

which further reduces the exponent to p −2. Such an estimate generally fails without the external field,
particularly in the small-velocity regime. We refer to Section 4 for further details.
• W 1,p–αC1
x,v bootstrap and C1
v estimate without kinetic weight
To control the second derivative ∥∇2
xϕh∥L∞
x
in the three-dimensional domain, weighted C1 regularity
remains necessary, as shown in Lemma 18 and Lemma 19. To circumvent the aforementioned difficulty with
∥∇3
xϕh∥L∞
x , our key observation is that the W 1,p estimate allows us to implement a W 1,p–αC1 bootstrap
argument, converting point-wise regularity into W 1,p control. This argument avoids integration by parts and
therefore does not require second derivatives of X(s). We note that this bootstrap argument shares the spirit
of the L2–L∞argument in [23], which applied to the double Duhamel formula without taking derivatives.
As a key component of this bootstrap argument, when the external potential field ϕE dominates near the
boundary, distance information can be incorporated into the kinetic weight αh (see Remark 3). Such a weight
was introduced in [2] for local-in-time solutions, remaining constant at a distance δ from the boundary while
compensating for the singularity n(xb) · vb near the grazing set γ0. To adapt this weight to the stationary
and global setting, we observe that, due to the presence of ϕE, the characteristic can stay near the boundary
only for a finite time, as shown in (2.35) in Lemma 5. This observation yields control of the variation of ϕE
along the characteristic in Lemma 13. Consequently, the nonlocal-to-local estimate in Lemma 17 remains
valid for large-time integration.
Furthermore, justifying dynamical stability requires a C1
v estimate without kinetic weight (see Remark
5). This is not generally true without an external field ∇xϕE. In our setting, the external field provides
control of tb via the singularity |n(x) · v|, as shown in (2.33). This estimate is crucial in controlling the
v-derivative of the characteristic in Lemma 3, where the denominator singularity is compensated by tb. We
refer to Section 5 for further details.

1.5. Outline of the paper. The rest of the paper is organized as follows: We collect several preliminary
estimates in Section 2, including properties of the characteristics, the collision operators, and the kinetic
weight. Sections 3, 4, and 5 establish a priori estimates for the stationary VPB system: the L2–L∞estimate,
the W 1,p estimate, and the α-C1
x,v and C1
v estimates without kinetic weight, respectively. In Section 6, we
prove the uniqueness of stationary solutions under a smallness condition on the unweighted C1
v estimate.
Based on the estimates and the uniqueness argument in Sections 3–6, Section 7 constructs stationary solutions
via a sequential scheme. For clarity, the a priori estimates, the well-posedness, and the existence argument
are presented separately. Finally, Section 8 studies the dynamical problem around the stationary solution
and establishes dynamical stability as well as uniform-in-time regularity, as stated in Theorem 2.
1.6. Notation. We introduce the following notational conventions. The relation f ≲g (resp. f ≳g) denotes
that there exists a constant C > 0 such that f ≤Cg (resp. f ≥Cg). The notation ∂x,vf denotes either a first-
order spatial derivative or a first-order velocity derivative of f. Thus, |∂x,vf| ≲1 means |∂xf| + |∂vf| ≲1,
and |∂x,vf| ≲|∂x,vg| means |∂xf| + |∂vf| ≲|∂xg| + |∂vg|. The operator I denotes the identity operator.

Lemma 13 (Velocity Lemma). There exists a constant C0 = C0(CE, Ω) > 0 such that for any t−tb ≤s ≤t,

4.
A priori W 1,p estimate for p < 3
In this section, we establish a priori weighted W 1,p estimate for p < 3 in Proposition 7. This is one of the
key ingredients in the well-posedness analysis in Sections 6 and 7. Another key set of C1 estimates will be
derived in Section 5.
Throughout this section, when applying the lemmas from Section 2, we replace f, ϕf by the stationary
solution h, ϕh defined in (1.7), and for the kinetic weight lemmas from Section 2.3, we replace α(t, x, v) by
the stationary weight αh(x, v) defined in (1.12).

5.
A priori weighted C1
x,v estimate and C1
v estimate without weight
In this section, we establish weighted C1
x,v regularity and C1
v regularity without the αh-weight in Propo-
sition 8 and Proposition 9, respectively.
Similar to Section 4, the stationary setting h, ϕh, αh(x, v) defined in (1.7) and (1.12) are adopted while
applying lemmas from Section 2. Moreover, we impose the same a priori assumption (4.1) as in Proposition
7. The smallness of ∇xϕh(x) in (4.1) ensures the existence of Ωδ such that (2.58) is satisfied.

6. Uniqueness of steady solution
In this section, we establish the uniqueness of the stationary problem (1.7) in Proposition 10. The proof
relies on the a priori L2 −L∞estimate and W 1,p estimate established in Sections 3 and 4, respectively.

motivates the derivation of the unweighted C1
v estimate in Proposition 9.

argument developed here will be adapted in the well-posedness and existence of the stationary problem in
Section 7.

The proof of Theorem 11 proceeds in two steps.
• In Step 1, we introduce a prescribed function g(x, v). Proposition 12 shows that the steady problem
(7.49)–(7.52), involving a sufficiently small constant 0 < λ ≪1, admits a unique solution.
We
remark that the inclusion of a small λ ≪1 is in the same spirit as in [12], where λ was used to
handle the ℓ-iteration of Kℓ.
• In Step 2, Proposition 15 applies an induction argument to extend the result from λ to 1. By
replacing the prescribed function g with the sequence {hℓ}∞
ℓ=0 in (7.1), the well-posedness of the
problem is established.

Remark 6. The system (7.49)-(7.52) is a Vlasov-Boltzmann system with a given potential field ∇x(ϕg +ϕE)
and additional source terms. Therefore, the well-posedness of the problem does not require regularity estimates
for h, and we only need to control the term −λ(v · ∇xϕh)e−ϕE/2√µ on the right-hand side.

8. Dynamical problem
In this section, we prove Theorem 2, which establishes the existence, uniqueness, dynamical stability,
and positivity of solutions to the dynamical problem (1.16). Theorem 2 also implies the positivity of the
stationary solution Fs = e−ϕEµ + e−ϕE/2√µh in Theorem 1.
We refer to Remark 4 and the proof of
Theorem 2 for further discussion.
Throughout this section, when applying the lemmas from Section 2, we treat f and ϕf as the dynamical
solution defined in (1.17), and for the kinetic weight lemmas from Section 2.3, we replace α(t, x, v) by the
dynamical weight αf(t, x, v) defined in (1.18).
Moreover, we let (h, ϕh) be a solution to the stationary
problem (1.7), satisfying the conclusions of Theorem 1. In addition, we assume the inflow condition (1.14),
the compatibility condition f0|γ−= 0, and the smallness condition in (1.19) from Theorem 2.
This section is organized as follows. Similar to the stationary problem, Sections 8.1 and 8.2 establish the a
priori L2–L∞estimate and the a priori regularity estimate, respectively. To conclude the well-posedness, we
apply the local argument in [2] and establish local well-posedness in Section 8.3. Finally, in Section 8.4, we
combine the a priori estimates, the local well-posedness, and a continuity argument to conclude the global
well-posedness and complete the proof of Theorem 2.
8.1. A priori L2 −L∞estimate. In this section, we establish the a priori L2–L∞estimate for solutions to
the dynamical problem (1.17). The argument is analogous to the L2–L∞estimate for the stationary problem
developed in Section 3.
To derive the estimate, we introduce the exponential time weight eλt into (1.17). More precisely, we
consider the equation for eλtf obtained from (1.17):
∂t(eλtf) −λeλtf + v · ∇x(eλtf) −∇x(ϕf + ϕE) · ∇v(eλtf) + v · ∇xϕf
2
eλtf + e−ϕEL(eλtf)
= −eλte−ϕE/2v · ∇x(ϕf −ϕh)√µ −eλt v · ∇x(ϕf −ϕh)
2
h + eλt∇x(ϕf −ϕh) · ∇vh
+ eλte−ϕE/2[Γ(f, f) + Γ(f, h) + Γ(h, f)].
(8.1)
We first show an energy estimate on the microscopic component (I −P)eλtf.

positivity of the solution. To prove the continuity, it suffices to prove that wf, αf∂x,vf, and ∇vf are continuous
in time t. Indeed, these three terms are represented via the Duhamel formula along characteristics, as in
(8.56) and Proposition 20. The continuity then follows by comparing the representations at times t and t−δ
and letting δ →0, which yields continuity in time. For a similar argument, we refer to Step 9 in Theorem 2
of [3]. The unique solution F = √µg is non-negative since (8.52) preserves positivity.

x,v + ∥w˜θαg∂x,vg(nT ′)∥L∞
x,v + ∥w˜θ∇vg(nT ′)∥L∞
x,v < |C7|n−1|C6|nC5.
(8.58)
This establishes the global well-posedness and the L∞
x,v estimate (1.20).
Note that the regularity estimate (8.58) grows exponentially in time. To justify the uniform-in-time bound
(1.21), we turn to the a priori estimate in Proposition 20. The continuity of g established in Proposition
21 implies the continuity of ∥w˜θ∇vf(t)∥L∞
x,v, ∥w˜θ∂x,vf(t)∥Lp
x,v, ∥w˜θαf∂x,vf(t)∥L∞
x,v. Applying the continuity
argument on ∥∇2
xϕf∥L∞
t,x together with Lemma 19, we obtain (8.19). Hence, all assumptions in Proposition
20 are satisfied. Consequently, the uniform-in-time estimates (8.20) and (1.21) follow from Proposition 20.
Finally, positivity holds for the local solution on [0, T ′] by Proposition 21. By induction, for every n ∈N,
positivity also holds on the interval [(n −1)T ′, nT ′]. Therefore, the global solution satisfies F(t, x, v) ≥0.
This, together with the asymptotic stability of the dynamical solution F(t, x, v), implies the positivity of the
stationary solution Fs(x, v) ≥0 in Theorem 1. This completes the proof of Theorem 2.
□
